We saw the warriors then. Straight into the village clearing they strode, Geipgos at their head and his son Slagoon walking proudly at his side. They were flourishing their spears and shouting, and the sky glow was bright on their green-bronze shoulders, and was mirrored in their eyes.
I had been right in my prediction. In less than an hour the rumblings ceased and the ground stopped trembling. The fieriness vanished from the sky. But for three days the warriors pursued the lizards across the crater’s rim, descending into the smoke-filled clefts where for centuries they had nested and multiplied, routing and shattering them until Dracona was cleansed and a brave new dawn broke over Geipgos’ unbowed head.
I stood with Kallatah on a cloud-wreathed peak staring down.
“When man is free to shape his own destiny,” I said, “civilization does not beat its shining wings in vain.

===Chinese===

蜥蜴们徒劳地想飞，响起一片连绵的乱爬乱扑之声。有什么把它们弄残、囚在了棚屋之间那片火纹地带里，它们逃不出去。
我并不知道接下来会怎样。我不确定对蜥蜴的第一击从哪里下手——是心智的张力先升满，还是物质的力先炸开、把那些畜生崩碎。
对骚灵你永远拿不准。他们思想与情绪的巨浪在他们心里乱流激荡，何况又掺进一条催眠后指令——
我们没有等多久。一条蜥蜴尖啸着高高跃起，被掼回一棵树上，力道猛得它落地时连垂死的痉挛都没来得及抽一下。它躺的地方起了一蓬火，围着它跳舞、闪烁。另一条蜥蜴被高高擎起，全身一阵可怕的痉挛性收缩，打着旋飞出去。
其余蜥蜴的乱爬更疯了，变成一场骇人的拧动和蠕挤，寒气顺着我的脊梁一路跑。一条蜥蜴爆开了，爆得吓人。它后腿直立时胸口整个被轰掉，被一道旋卷的火螺旋带走。别的像被看不见的利爪撕开，压扁、碾碎、撕成碎片。
这时我们看见武士们了。他们大步走进村里的空地，盖普戈斯领头，儿子斯拉贡骄傲地走在旁边。他们挥着长矛呐喊，天光照亮他们古铜绿的肩膀，映在他们眼里。
我的预测没错。不到一个钟头，轰隆停了，地不再颤。天上的火退了。可整整三天，武士们追杀蜥蜴越过火山口缘，下到烟腾的裂谷——几个世纪来它们在那里筑巢、繁衍——剿杀、击碎，直到德拉科纳荡涤一清，一个崭新的勇敢的黎明在盖普戈斯不曾低下的头上破晓。
我同卡拉塔站在一座云绕的峰顶俯瞰。
“当人类得以亲手塑造自己的命运，”我说，“文明的闪亮翅膀就不是白扑扇的。他们如今会大步向前，迷信的轭永远从他们颈上摘除了。”
“多亏了你，”卡拉塔说。
“预测火山喷发的准确时刻，不算太难，”我说。“至于那条催眠后指令——你也能做到。”
卡拉塔转过来看我，眼睛异样地亮。“塔罗·哈根，”她说。“我要告诉你一件事。从见你的头一刻起，我就知道你是个*男人*。”
我瞪着她，心里犯疑。

===Original===

They will go forward boldly now, with the yoke of superstition forever removed from their necks.”
“Thanks to you,” Kallatah said.
“It was not too difficult to predict the exact moment of the volcano’s eruption,” I said. “As for the post-hypnotic command﻿—you could have done that too.”
Kallatah turned and looked at me, her eyes strangely luminous. “Taro Hargon,” she said. “I am going to tell you something. From the first moment I saw you I knew that you were a *man*.”
I stared at her, wondering.
“It takes the courage and daring and resourcefulness of the Earthborn to do what you did. The Guiding Council realized that we on Tragor had need of the Earthborn too. Our heritage had worn too thin. So they selected a man of Terra with truly great gifts of body and mind to guide us all﻿—”
I grew alarmed, still wondering how insecure my secret had become.
“Oh, you returned for a lark. Took to wandering again, as an Earthborn trader. For an hour and a day.”
She laughed and her hands were suddenly busy at the flaps of my weather jacket, peeling them back to bring the shining insignia into view.
“Supreme Councillor and Guide,” she whispered. “I knew it from the first.”
“You knew﻿—”
“Oh, my darling, yes. I guessed, I knew. In the hushed great halls the footsteps of a man like you were solely needed. The Guiding Council had vision and strength too﻿—the courage to break through all taboos and seek the one right man for a task no other man could do.
“Naturally you are human still. In my presence you felt at first a certain shyness. I could see that you were searching inwardly for flaws that might have made you seem unworthy in my eyes. It was a boyish, foolish trait﻿—and from that moment I loved you with all my heart. To be great and doubt one’s greatness is the surest path to a woman’s heart.”
I reached out and took her into my arms. “No man is a safe guide when he walks alone,” I said. “It is time that the Councillor took a wife.”
“Yes,” she agreed. “It is time.”
There was silence on the peak as we stared down together into the shining valley far below.

===Chinese===

“干成你干的事，要的是地球人的胆魄、锐气和机变。指导委员会也明白，我们特拉戈同样需要地球人。我们的家底磨得太薄了。于是他们选中了一位地球人，一位身心禀赋真正卓绝的男人，来引领我们全体——”
我发慌了，还在想我的秘密到底漏成了什么样。
“哦，你是回来散心的。又当起了地球行商，四处漂一漂。图一个钟头、一天的快活。”
她笑了，两手忽然忙起来，扒开我风衣的翻领，把那枚闪亮的徽章翻出来。
“最高议长兼引路人，”她低声说。“我从头一眼就知道了。”
“你知道——”
“哦，亲爱的，是。我猜到，我知道。肃穆的大厅里，独独需要的是一个像你这样的男人的脚步声。指导委员会也有眼光、有魄力——敢破掉一切禁忌，为一件谁也办不成的差事，找到那个唯一对的人。
“你当然还是人。在我面前，你起初还有几分害羞。我看得出你在心里找自己的短处，怕它们叫你在我眼里配不上。那是一股孩子气的傻劲儿——从那一刻起，我就全心全意爱你了。伟大而怀疑自己的伟大，是通向一颗女人心最稳的路。”
我伸出手把她揽进怀里。“独自走路的人，当不得稳当的引路人，”我说。“议长该娶妻了。”
“是，”她应道。“是时候了。”
峰顶一片寂静，我们一同俯望脚下远处那道发亮的溪谷。

## Preview / 预映
===Original===

“What every discerning theatergoer is searching for,” said Mr. Scanlon, “is a kind of sixth-sense heightening of perception. I choose to call it a superseding experience﻿—a blotting out of the mundane and familiar and the substitution of the totally unexpected. In short, a superior vision of reality. Stella, you see, is a ‘stripped’ actress.”
Mr. Scanlon looked about him, and smiled. “No, gentlemen. I’m not being Rabelaisian, or thinking of the burlesque stage. She’s a ‘stripped’ actress in the sense that the kind of realism I’ve been talking about is her guiding star. Everything else﻿—every shred of romantic or sentimental tinsel﻿—has been stripped away.”
“But, Mr. Scanlon! Surely﻿—”
“Gentlemen, I think you’ll agree that her first liaison with Hollywood has carried her to heights undreamed of in your philosophy﻿—if drama critics can legitimately be said to possess a philosophy.”
“But, Mr. Scanlon﻿—”
“Gentlemen, be patient. Relax. You’ll never see another preview like this, and you must enjoy it to the full. There go the lights.”
The lights dimmed and the long, cinemascopic screen brimmed with a deep-toned miracle of radiance.
Into the Black Hills on a white charger rode Robert Mitchum. The epoch which was to resound with his deeds of valor was not immediately determinable. But from the peaceful aspect of the landscape everyone took it for granted that it was BC (Before Custer)﻿—until the new techniques of sound dispersal which convey an illusion of torment in depth went into action. From every nook and cranny of the theater rifles cracked in earsplitting synchronization.
It was no run-of-the-hill Western. Every part was played to perfection, against the kind of historically realistic background that had made *Shane* and a scant dozen lesser pictures classics of their kind.
The critics flanking Mr. Scanlon realized this, and unlimbering their fastidious brows they started scribbling.
“There she is,” said Mr. Scanlon. “Gentlemen, this should be your most rewarding moment.”
Into the center of that scenic magnificence stepped Stella. Her blonde loveliness was peculiarly her very own, and it was easy to see that everything was just right for her.
“No powder, no cream, no lipstick,” murmured Mr. Scanlon.
“But surely, Mr. Scanlon, she’s made up for the screen﻿—”
“Historically makeup would have been an anachronism,” Mr. Scanlon pointed out.

===Chinese===

“每一个有鉴赏力的戏剧观众所追寻的，”斯坎伦先生说，“是一种第六感式的知觉提升。我姑且称之为一种‘取而代之的体验’——把平庸熟悉的东西涂掉，换上全然意料之外的东西。简言之，一种更高一级的真实之视象。斯特拉，你们知道，是个‘剥离’的女演员。”
斯坎伦先生环顾四周，微微一笑。“不，先生们。我不是在讲拉伯雷，也没想滑稽戏的台子。说她是‘剥离’的女演员，意思是：我方才讲的那路现实主义是她的指路星。其余的一切——每一丝浪漫的、伤感的俗金箔——都剥掉了。”
“可是，斯坎伦先生！ surely——”
“先生们，我想你们会同意，她同好莱坞的第一次结缘，已经把她带到了你们的哲学做梦也够不着的高处——如果戏剧评论家也配说有哲学的话。”
“可是，斯坎伦先生——”
“先生们，耐心。放松。这样的预映你们再看不着第二场了，你们得尽情受用。灯灭了。”
灯暗下来，宽阔的深景银幕里涨满一泓深沉的、光华的奇迹。
罗伯特·米彻姆骑一匹白骏，驰进黑山。他的武功将要震响的那个时代，一时还看不出来。可一路山川太平景象，人人想当然当它是“前卡斯特纪”（BC）——直到那种营造纵深苦难幻觉的全新声场技术开了工。剧院的每一个旮旯犄角，步枪震耳欲聋地齐响。
这不是西部片里的泛泛之作。每一场戏都演到家，衬底的历史真实感，正是《原野奇侠》和寥寥十几部次一等片子赖以成为经典的那一种。
斯坎伦先生两翼的评论家们看出了这一点，舒开挑剔的眉头，动笔疾书。
“她来了，”斯坎伦先生说。“先生们，这一刻该是你们最值当的一刻。”
斯特拉踏进那壮丽画境的正中央。她那金发的可爱是她独有的，一眼就看得出来，万事都正合她的身。
“不扑粉，不抹油，不涂口红，”斯坎伦先生喃喃。
“可是 sure，斯坎伦先生，上银幕她总要化妆的——”
“就史实而言，化妆是个时代错置，”斯坎伦先生指出。

===Original===

“The women of that age had to get along as best they could. Remember Calamity Jane? She was as ugly as a warthog, but her lovers were legion.”
There was a brief dimming of the radiance. And then, into the Black Hills on a pulsing shape of darkness rode﻿—
“It’s Humpty Dumpty!” someone gasped.
“No.”
“No, it’s got tentacles. Can’t you see? They must have been shooting one of those science fiction pictures on another set. One of the extras must have strayed by accident﻿—”
“And he’s waving them. Look!”
“Good grief, how did that bomb crater get there?”
“It’s unbelievable! She’s changing into a seal!”
The divine Stella was indeed changing. But no seal﻿—or mermaid﻿—had ever gone flapping over the Western hills on eight dangling appendages, blue-green in hue.
Of course the hills were no longer the Black Hills of the Dakotas. But no one could ask Mr. Scanlon about that because he had disappeared. One moment he had been leaning forward in his seat, alert and smiling. Then he was gone. He had simply vanished﻿—dwindled and faded into nothingness like an unstable isotope assailed by some invisible energy source.
No narrative is complete if it does not terminate with some sort of explanation. But how could there be one when no man or woman on Earth would ever know where Mr. Scanlon went, or why he had insisted on a preview in the first place?
How could there be one when there was no one to remember﻿—no one to recall how *They* landed in numbers from their flat, saucer-like ships exactly one week later, and took care to strip from the minds of men all proudly treasured knowledge of the many-splendored things of Earth?

===Chinese===

“那个时代的女人只能凑合着过。记得灾星简吗？她丑得像头疣猪，可她的情人成军。”
光华暗了一暗。然后，一团搏动的黑影驰进黑山——
“是蛋头先生！”有人倒抽气。
“不是。”
“不，它长着触手。看不见吗？准是另一个棚在拍那种科幻片。一个群众演员迷了路误闻进来的——”
“它还在挥那些触手呢。看！”
“天哪，那个弹坑是打哪儿来的？”
“没法相信！她变成一头海豹了！”
神圣的斯特拉确实在变。可从来没有哪头海豹——或人鱼——挥着八条垂荡的、蓝绿色的附肢，扑扇着越过西部的群山。
当然，那些山也已经不是达科他的黑山了。可没人能就这个去问斯坎伦先生，因为他不见了。前一刻他还前倾在座位里，警醒，含笑。转眼他没了。他就是消失了——缩小、褪色，没入虚无，像一个遭到某种不可见能源轰击的不稳定同位素。
一则叙事若不收个交代，就不算完。可这一桩哪来的交代呢？地球上没有任何男人女人会知道斯坎伦先生去了哪里，也不知道他当初为什么执意要办这场预映。
哪来的交代呢——已经没有人记得了。没有人记得整整一个星期之后，*他们*乘着那一条条扁平的、碟形飞船，成群落了下来，还细心地把人类头脑里那些引以为宝的知识——关于地球上种种光华事物的知识——剥了个干净。

## Young Man with a Trumpet / 少年与小号
===Original===

When Man left Earth forever the animals still carried on proudly.
They knew, of course, that they could never hope to equal the Great One’s shining accomplishments. But even the warthog and the lowly cricket were determined to avoid his mistakes and the tragic impulsiveness which had led to his defeat on a battleground of his own choosing.
It may seem surprising to some, but what they missed most of all was music. It was more the pity because animals in general have an excellent idea of pitch and can swing and sway in quite miraculous fashion to nature’s inimitable rhythms.
They finally decided that something should be done about it. So they went into a huddle and came up with an astounding interrogative aphorism: *Why not try?*
The leading spirit in this conclave of daring was, of course, the ass. He stood up on his hindlegs and gave vent to a tremendous bray. This was the more remarkable because﻿—unlike his far less intelligent cousin, the horse﻿—the ass has little talent for vocal improvisation.
It was a most commendable effort but it failed dismally. A bray is not music. It may have some of the qualities of music, but a single chord, however magnificently sustained, does not make for rapture on a tonal plane.
In fact, regrettable as it may seem, the fox and the warthog held their ears, the skunk exuded an unforgivably unpleasant effluvium, and the prairie dog darted like a streak of midsummer lightning into his burrow.
The wolf tried next. His howl was distinctly on the ferocious side and it contributed nothing to the pleasures of the moment, even though there was an undercurrent of primitive amorousness in it which should have delighted the females of his species.
“You’ll have to do better than that!” the crocodile said, a comment which could have been put down to pure masculine pique, for the crocodile is notoriously sluggish in his mating habits.
The bullfrog tried next, as if to prove that, while not exactly hairy-chested, he was in all respects the exact opposite of the crocodile. The cacophony of croakings which emanated from his throat was undeniably impressive, but a tonepoem with a buzzsaw accompaniment is, at best, a contradiction in terms.
“Heard melodies are sweet, but those unheard are sweeter,” quoted the warthog, his uncouth swiftness-on-the-trigger debasing the lines with an ironic implication never intended by Keats.

===Chinese===

人类永远离开地球之后，动物们依旧骄傲地过活着。
它们当然知道，伟大者那些闪光的成就，它们永远别指望赶得上。可就连疣猪和卑贱的蟋蟀，也决意避开他的种种错误，避开那股悲剧性的冲动——正是那冲动叫他败在了他自己选定的战场上。
有人也许觉得奇怪：它们最怀念的竟是音乐。这就更可惜了，因为动物大都有极好的音准，能跟着大自然无可仿效的节拍，摇摆出相当奇迹的舞姿。
它们终于决定，此事总得想个办法。于是它们聚成一堆，凑出了一句惊人的疑问式格言：*何不试试？*
这场胆大聚会的领头魂灵，当然是驴。他后腿站立起来，放出一声惊天动地的嘶叫。这就更了不起了，因为驴——不像他那远不怎么聪明的表亲马——素来不长于即兴开嗓。
这一番苦心可嘉，却败得一塌糊涂。嘶叫不是音乐。它或许带几分音乐的品性，可一个和弦，无论支撑得多么辉煌，在音调的层面上造不出迷醉。
事实上，遗憾归遗憾，狐狸和疣猪捂住了耳朵，臭鼬放出了一股不可原谅的恶臭，草原犬鼠像一道仲夏的闪电，嗖地钻回了洞。
下一个试的是狼。他的嚎叫分明杀气偏重，全不增进此刻的雅兴，尽管里头有一股原始求欢的暗流，本该叫他族里的雌性心花怒放。
“你们得拿出比这强点的玩意儿！”鳄鱼说。这句评语尽可以记在纯粹的雄性醋意账上，因为鳄鱼的交游习性是出了名的迟钝。
下一个试的是牛蛙，仿佛要证明：自己虽谈不上毛胸膛，却样样都是鳄鱼的反面。从他喉咙里涌出的一串聒噪确实震撼，可一首配着圆锯的音诗，往好里说，也是个自相矛盾。
“听见的旋律甜，听不见的更甜，”疣猪引经据典，他那上膛飞快的粗嗓子，把这几句诗糟蹋出一层济慈绝无此意的讽刺。

===Original===

“A legacy of song and story as impressive as Man’s should be the exclusive heritage of the elite,” said the Hornbill, with a patronizing, scholarly vanity.
His own contribution was a tragic flop. It was artificial and stilted, and it provoked the hyena to gales of laughter. Almost instantly the hyena realized that he had betrayed himself, and could have bitten his tongue out. He had intended to improvise out of character, with a melodious persuasiveness, but the laughter was accepted by everyone as an ordeal that had to be endured and could now be dismissed as a fait accompli.
“I’m glad that’s over,” breathed the Spoonbill, and went into a solo that made even the Hornbill shudder, despite their undoubted kinship as members of a team whose erudition and bald-domed superiority could not be challenged.
It was now the turn of the big cats. The less said about their roars the better. The lion’s was fiercely belligerent, the tiger’s playful and beguiling, for contrary to popular belief the tiger is an amiable beast. But how could such formidable vocalisms conjure up visions Mozartian?
Then it happened. A small, wiry figure arose and without even awaiting his turn lifted to his lips an incredible instrument of sound. It was fashioned of brass, and it flared at its tip like an earphone﻿—the earphone of some pitifully ancient individual who has lost all touch with reality, and thinks of sound as a crutch.
But the instrument was neither an earphone nor a crutch. The fox with his native sagacity and the Hornbill with his learning and knowledge of prehistory recognized it for what it was.
It was a trumpet, and﻿—it was golden. It never had been brass except in a wearisome literal sense which did not matter at all. So golden indeed was it that there came from it now, like a banner resplendently unfurled, an incomparable salute to the dawn.
Never had man dared to dream of such music. It was as if Ulysses, lashed to his mast, had burst his bonds at last, and was with swift breast strokes approaching the island of his heart’s desire.
For this was siren music. This was the music of the spheres. It was the music almost of Man dying young﻿—Shelley in the blue Mediterranean, Bix Beiderbecke in canyons of steel, his last high imperishable note winging its way into the blue infinite.
Almost, but not quite, it was Man music.

===Chinese===

“一份像人类那样可观的歌与传说的遗产，理应是精英的专产，”犀鸟说，带着居高临下的、学者式的自负。
他自己的贡献是一场惨败。造作，僵硬，惹得鬣狗一阵狂笑。鬣狗几乎立刻醒悟自己露了馅，恨不得把舌头咬下来。他本打算跳出本色、即兴一段婉转的说服，可这阵笑被大家当成了必经的一场煎熬，如今既成事实，揭过去了。
“总算熬过去了，”篦鹭舒了口气，来了一段独奏，连犀鸟都打了个寒噤——尽管作为一队，它们那不容挑战的博学和秃顶的优越感素来沆瀣一气。
现在轮到大猫们。它们的吼还是少提为妙。狮子的凶狠好斗，老虎的顽皮撩人——因为同世俗之见相反，老虎是种和气的兽。可这样威猛的嗓门，怎么变得出莫扎特的幻境？
然后，事情发生了。一个小小的、精瘦的身影站起来，连自己那一轮都不等，就把一件不可思议的发声器举到唇边。它是铜做的，管口张开像一只耳机——某个可怜巴巴的老古董的耳机，那人同现实早已脱节，把声音当作一根拐棍。
可这件乐器既不是耳机，也不是拐棍。狐狸凭他天生的明察，犀鸟凭他的学问和史前知识，认出了它是什么。
是一支小号——而且是金子的。它从来不是铜的，除非在一种全不相干的、叫人厌倦的字面意义上。它是那么金，此刻从它里面涌出来的，像一面辉煌展开的大旗，是一声献给黎明、无可比拟的敬礼。
人类做梦也不敢梦这样的音乐。仿佛尤利西斯挣断了桅杆上的绑绳，正以飞快的蛙泳，向他心之所系的小岛游去。
因为这是塞壬的音乐。这是诸天球的音乐。这几乎是人类夭折的音乐——雪莱在蓝色的地中海，比克斯·拜德贝克在钢铁的峡谷，他最后那个高高的、不朽的音符，正振翅飞进蓝色的无穷。
几乎，但不全是，这是人的音乐。

===Original===

By dint of long, patient practice it had simply taken on those shadings of absolute perfection﻿—the “little more”﻿—that are the hallmarks of genius in its lonely and solitary pilgrimage under the stars.
Was it Eddington who first affirmed that if you set a monkey to pounding on a typewriter through all eternity he will eventually write *Hamlet* or *King Lear*? The statement has since found innumerable supporters who have paraphrased it in one way or another.
But this was not Shakespeare. This was music, sensuous and unadorned.
The monkey lowered his horn, and stood very still and straight and proud while thunderous applause echoed and reechoed through the glade and the star-bright meadows beyond.
He had known all along that he could do it.

===Chinese===

凭着漫长而耐心的练习，它无非是把那些绝对完美的着色——那“多出的一点”——练上了身。那是一个天才在星群之下孤独朝圣的印记。
是不是爱丁顿头一个断言的：让一只猴子在打字机前敲上永恒，它终会敲出《哈姆雷特》或《李尔王》？此论随后赢得了无数拥趸，这样那样地转述。
可这不是莎士比亚。这是音乐，肉感而不加修饰。
猴子放下号，笔直而骄傲地站着，雷鸣般的掌声在林间空地和空地外星光灿亮的草地上回荡，再回荡。
他一直都知道，自己办得到。

## The Spectacles / 眼镜
===Original===

It was a most delightful day, with a crisp autumn tang in the air and Willie felt a joyful lift throughout his entire being. It was forbidden, of course, and he had no right at all to even enter the hall and go romping over its mottled flagstones toward the case and the spectacles. But he simply didn’t care.
“Oh, happy me!” he thought. “Oh, joyous day! What do I care if I am caught and punished?” He was very little and the case was enormous and it glittered in the sunlight from a picture-window that overlooked a many-splendored bay. Beyond the window gulls dipped and wheeled and far in the distance a black buoy rolled with the sea’s resistless surge.
It was easy enough to climb into the case and emerge with the spectacles. The glass was shattered over half its length, and the pale mummified brow upon which the spectacles reposed could offer no resistance. Not even the mysterious emanations of thought which had once issued from it could daunt in retrospect so small a thief on such a day as this.
“Oh, glorious, carefree, wonderful me!” intoned Willie.
Into the case he crawled and out of it he strode triumphant, with the spectacles perched on the bridge of his gladsomely vibrating nose.
“Where will I sit?” he asked himself, looking up first at the sky above, and then down with a pensive wink at the waters which covered the earth.
“Right here by the window. Why not? Big dreams, wonderful dreams, must have room to stretch their limbs and go striding over land and sea like giants in search of their lady loves.”
Willie sat down, crossed his small legs and looked out through the spectacles at the sea and the sky.
Almost immediately a ship came into view. It was a very large ship and passengers thronged the rails and white handkerchiefs fluttered in the breeze, and there was a great shouting from the decks and a rejoicing that could not possibly have existed at all if Willie had not been sitting there to take part in it.
“Hello, Willie!” came in a rising chorus. “Isn’t it a glorious day? We’re heading straight into the sunrise, Willie. Believe it or not, there are palm-fringed islands out there just begging to be explored, and brown-skinned women who would die immediately if no one ever came to make love to them.
“Think of it, Willie. This big round Earth is ours to enjoy for ever and ever.”
“I know,” said Willie, and waved back. Or rather, he shouted it.

===Chinese===

那是个再可人不过的日子，空气里带着秋天脆生生的凉意，威利觉得浑身从上到下都是欢喜。这当然是犯禁的，他连进大厅、踩着斑驳的石板一路蹦到柜子和那副眼镜跟前去的权利都没有。可他压根儿不在乎。
“哦，幸福的找！”他想。“哦，快活的日子！就算被抓住、受罚，又关我什么事？”他那么小，柜子那么大，阳光从一面俯瞰着绚烂海湾的景观窗照进来，柜子熠熠发光。窗外鸥鸟翻飞盘旋，远处一只黑色浮标随着大海不可抗拒的涌浪滚动。
爬进柜子取出眼镜，容易得很。玻璃碎了一半，托着眼镜的那具灰白的干尸额头，无力做半点抵抗。就连它生前发出的那种种神秘的思维辐射， retrospect 起来，也吓不倒今天这样一个顺手的小贼。
“哦，光荣、无忧、妙极了的找！”威利唱着。
他爬进柜子，又神气活现地大步跨出来，眼镜架在他欢喜得直颤的鼻梁上。
“我坐哪儿呢？”他问自己，先抬头望望天，又若有所思地眨眨眼，望望漫过大地的那片水。
“就坐窗边吧。干吗不？大梦，妙梦，得有地方伸胳膊伸腿，得像巨人一样跨过陆地和海洋，去寻它们的情人。”
威利坐下，盘起小小的腿，隔着那副眼镜望海和天。
几乎立刻，一艘船进了视野。一艘很大的船，乘客挤满栏杆，白手帕在风里飘动，甲板上一片欢呼——那阵欢庆，若不是威利坐在那儿参与，根本就不会存在。
“哈罗，威利！”一片升起来的合唱。“多美的一天哪！我们直朝日出开去，威利。信不信由你，外头有镶棕榈边的海岛，正求着人去探，有棕色皮肤的女人，要是没人去爱她们，她们立刻就会死掉。
“想想吧，威利。这颗圆圆的大地球，归咱们世世代代享用。”
“我知道，”威利说，挥手回礼。更准确说，他是喊出来的。

===Original===

“I understand! You don’t have to tell me! I know what it means to be a man, and young and in love. I even know what you’re thinking. The women you are holding in your arms wouldn’t seem half so wonderful if you couldn’t dream of brown-skinned girls too﻿—women you’ll never really meet, women who don’t even exist.”
“Sure, Willie, sure﻿—that’s it. But how did you know? How did you even guess? You’re just a﻿—”
“Willie, put those spectacles down this minute!” a familiar voice interrupted. “Take them off and give them to me. I’ll put them back, and then we’ll both have to be very humble, and hope that destruction won’t come upon us. You’ve done a terrible thing, Willie. It’s worse than a crime. It’s﻿—”
Willie jumped up and took the spectacles off, and gave them to the mother-model. She stood towering over him, sternly severe and reproving, her seven-foot android robot bulk blotting out the sunlight at her back.
“We were made to obey Man﻿—not to try to understand him!” she said.
“But he is gone forever now. He will never return.”
“It doesn’t matter. We were made to obey.”
Willie looked at the mummified figure in the case and his small conical head drooped in resignation. The autumn sky seemed suddenly gray and forbidding and the waters which covered the Earth had become a leaden expanse of emptiness.

===Chinese===

“我懂！不用你们告诉我！我知道做一个男人、一个恋爱中的年轻人是什么滋味。我连你们在想什么都知道。怀里搂着的那些女人，要是不能同时梦想棕色皮肤的姑娘——那些你永远真见不着的、甚至根本不存在的女人——她们就没那么美妙了。”
“对，威利，对——就是这么回事。可你怎么知道的？你怎么猜着的？你不过是个——”
“威利，马上把眼镜放下！”一个熟悉的声音打断。“摘下来给我。我把它放回去，然后咱们俩都得谦卑到底，祈求毁灭别落到咱们头上。你干了件可怕的事，威利。比罪还糟。它是——”
威利跳起来摘下眼镜，递给那个母亲型的机体。她 towering 在他上方，严厉峻责，她那七英尺高的仿生机身挡住了背后的太阳。
“我们被造出来是为了服从人——不是去试着理解他！”她说。
“可他现在永远走了。他再也不会回来了。”
“那不要紧。我们被造出来是为了服从。”
威利看看柜子里那具干尸，小小的锥形脑袋认命地垂下来。秋天的天忽然显得灰而凶，漫过大地的那片水，成了一大片铅色的空茫。
